<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct an Artin group on 116 generators that admits no proper, cocompact isometric action on a nonempty proper CAT(0) space. This refutes the CAT(0) conjecture for Artin groups.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is an Artin matrix $M$ on 116 generators, with every off-diagonal label in $\{2,3,\infty\}$, such that $A_M$ admits no geometric action. One explicit matrix is given in Definition <a href="#def:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:matrix">20</a>.
  - Explanation: An explicit Artin group refutes the universal geometric-action expectation in every dimension.
- **C2** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-comparison)): In a CAT(0) space, geodesic segments are unique and depend continuously on their endpoints. If $\alpha,\beta:[0,1]\to X$ are geodesics parametrized proportionally to length, then $$\label{eq:distance-convexity} d(\alpha(t),\beta(t)) \le (1-t)d(\alpha(0),\beta(0))+t d(\alpha(1),\beta(1)).$$ If $m$ is the midpoint of $[v,w]$, then $$\label{eq:midpoint-comparison} d(z,m)^2\le \frac{d(z,v)^2+d(z,w)^2}{2} -\frac{d(v,w)^2}{4}.$$ For any four points $p,q,r,s$, $$\label{eq:quadrilateral} d(p,r)^2+d(q,s)^2 \le d(p,q)^2+d(q,r)^2+d(r,s)^2+d(s,p)^2.$$
  - Explanation: Triangle comparison gives unique geodesics, continuous endpoint dependence and the distance inequalities used later.
- **C3** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-semisimple)): Suppose $G$ acts geometrically on $X$. Every $g\in G$ attains its minimum displacement, and $$\ell(g)=\min_{x\in X}d(x,gx).$$ If this number is positive, $g$ preserves a geodesic line and translates along it by $\ell(g)$. Moreover, $\ell(g)=0$ if and only if $g$ has finite order.
  - Explanation: A geometric action makes displacement minima attainable, identifies them with stable lengths and detects finite order.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-commuting-form)): For pairwise commuting isometries $g_1,\ldots,g_k$ of a CAT(0) space, the function $$v\longmapsto \ell(g^v),\qquad g^v=g_1^{v_1}\cdots g_k^{v_k},\qquad v\in\mathbb Z^k,$$ extends uniquely to a continuous seminorm on $\mathbb R^k$ whose square is a positive semidefinite quadratic form. For commuting $g,h$, put $$\label{eq:pairing-definition} \langle g,h\rangle =\frac{\ell(gh)^2-\ell(g)^2-\ell(h)^2}{2}.$$ These pairings are the bilinear form on every commuting family; in particular, $\langle g^i,h^j\rangle=ij\langle g,h\rangle$. Lengths and pairings are invariant under simultaneous conjugation.
  - Explanation: Commuting isometries determine a positive semidefinite quadratic form and a conjugation-invariant bilinear pairing.
- **C5** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-centralizer-character)): For a geometric action and a fixed $g\in G$, the function $$C_G(g)\longrightarrow\mathbb R,\qquad h\longmapsto\langle g,h\rangle$$ is a group homomorphism. Thus $\langle g,h_1h_2\rangle=\langle g,h_1\rangle+\langle g,h_2\rangle$ whenever both $h_1$ and $h_2$ commute with $g$.
  - Explanation: Pairing with a fixed element is additive on its whole centralizer, even when the other elements do not commute.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-braid-angle)): Suppose a group acting geometrically on a CAT(0) space contains elements $s_1,\ldots,s_{39}$ satisfying the $B_{40}$ relations, with $\ell(s_1^2)>0$. Then $$\label{eq:braid-angle-bound} \left(\frac{\ell((s_1s_2)^3)}{3\ell(s_1^2)}\right)^2 \ge\frac{143}{513}>\frac14,$$ and in particular $$\frac{\ell((s_1s_2)^3)}{3\ell(s_1^2)} =\frac{\ell((s_1s_2)^3)}{3\ell(s_2^2)}>\frac12.$$
  - Explanation: The long braid relations impose a strict lower threshold on the normalized central translation length.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-braid-centers)): The element $L_j$ commutes with $s_i$ for $i\le j-2$, and $Z_r$ commutes with every $s_i$ for $i<r$. In particular, $$Z_2=s_1^2,\qquad Z_3=(s_1s_2)^3.$$ The element $\delta=s_1\cdots s_{l-1}$ satisfies $$\label{eq:braid-shift} \delta s_i\delta^{-1}=s_{i+1}\qquad(1\le i\le l-2).$$ In any geometric action of the ambient group, $$\label{eq:braid-central-pairing} \langle s_i^2,Z_r\rangle=\frac{\ell(Z_r)^2}{N_r} \qquad(2\le r\le l,\ 1\le i<r).$$
  - Explanation: Recursive braid words centralize initial subblocks and give the shift and central-pairing identities.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-triangle-obstruction)): There is no geometric action of $T$ such that, for every $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$, $$\label{eq:strict-ratios} \frac{\ell(z_{uv})}{3\ell(u^2)} =\frac{\ell(z_{uv})}{3\ell(v^2)}>\frac12.$$
  - Explanation: The cyclic braid pairs cannot all satisfy the strict length ratios in a geometric action of the triangle group.
- **C9** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-orbit-control)): For a geometric action of $T$ and any base point $y$, there are constants $R,C<\infty$ such that $Ty$ is $R$-dense and $$\label{eq:orbit-control} d_T(s,t)\le C\bigl(1+d_Y(sy,ty)\bigr)\qquad(s,t\in T).$$
  - Explanation: Cocompactness supplies orbit density and properness bounds word distance by ambient displacement.
- **C10** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-mirror-action)): The formulas $$u\cdot\xi=e_{D_u}+r_u\xi\qquad(u=a,b,c)$$ define an affine isometric action of $T$ on $E$. If $V(t)=t\cdot0$, then $$\label{eq:mirror-lipschitz} \\|V(t)-V(s)\\|_1\le d_T(s,t).$$ For each $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$, the elements $u^2,z_{uv}$ act by pure translations with vectors $$\label{eq:mirror-translations} L_u=2e_{D_u},\qquad L_{uv}=2(e_{D_u}+e_{D_v}+e_{D_{uv}}),$$ where $D_{uv}$ is the third mirror at $D_u\cap D_v$. The three lines $D_{ab},D_{bc},D_{ca}$ are distinct from one another and from $D_a,D_b,D_c$.
  - Explanation: The mirror action satisfies the braid relations and separates squared-generator and center translation vectors.
- **C11** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-sector-data)): Each of these six seminorms is positive definite. Each sector angle $\theta$ satisfies $0<\theta<\pi/3$. The sectors glue isometrically along their shared rays, in the cyclic order $$\label{eq:ray-order} a^2,\ z_{ab},\ b^2,\ z_{bc},\ c^2,\ z_{ca},\ a^2.$$ Their radius-one disk $P$ is a topological disk with total angle $0<\Omega<2\pi$. It has a continuous injective map $v_*:P\to E$ whose value in a sector for $U=u^2,H=z_{uv}$ is $$\label{eq:separating-coordinates} v_*(\lambda,\mu)=\lambda L_u+\mu L_{uv},$$ and similarly with $v$ in place of $u$.
  - Explanation: Mirror independence makes the sector forms positive definite; the sectors assemble into a disk with an injective coordinate map.
- **C12** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-sector-maps)): There are continuous maps $f_n:P\to Y$, with $f_n(0)=y$, and a constant $C_0$ independent of $n,p$, such that $$\label{eq:grid-error} d_Y(f_n(p),t_n(p)y)\le C_0.$$ For two points in any one sector, $$\label{eq:sector-convergence} \frac{d_Y(f_n(p),f_n(p'))}{n} \longrightarrow\\|p-p'\\|_{U,H}$$ uniformly. In addition, uniformly on $P$, $$\label{eq:radial-coordinates} \frac{d_Y(y,f_n(p))}{n}\longrightarrow r(p), \qquad \frac{V(t_n(p))}{n}\longrightarrow v_*(p).$$ If $A_1,A_2\subset P$ are disjoint nonempty closed sets, then for some $\delta>0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, $$\label{eq:region-separation} d_Y(f_n(A_1),f_n(A_2))\ge\delta n.$$
  - Explanation: Geodesic interpolation produces uniform asymptotic sector maps and separates disjoint closed regions at large scale.
- **C13** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-outer-cap)): There is $\epsilon>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$, the map $f_n\|_{\partial P}$ extends to a second parameter disk whose entire image lies outside $B_Y(y,\epsilon n)$. Gluing the two parameter disks gives a continuous sphere map, still denoted $f_n$.
  - Explanation: The short boundary circuit can be coned to a disk whose image avoids the cone point at large scale.
- **C14** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-triangular-presentation)): For each $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$, introduce two private generators $x_{uv}$ and $h_{uv}$. Write $(x_1,x_2,x_3,h)=(u,v,x_{uv},h_{uv})$ within this block. A presentation of $T$ is given by the three relations $$\label{eq:triangle-relations} x_i x_{i+1}=h\qquad (i=1,2,3),$$ in each block, where the subscripts on the $x_i$ are read modulo three.
  - Explanation: Eliminating private generators recovers the original braid relations and proves equivalence of the triangular presentation.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-link-girth)): The link at every vertex of $\mathcal K$ has no circuit of length less than six, including circuits formed by parallel edges.
  - Explanation: The explicitly labelled vertex link has no short circuit, including any formed from parallel edges.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-sphere-counts)): For every combinatorial map from a finite triangulated oriented two-sphere to $\mathcal K$, and every individual target triangle $\tau$, the sum of the signs of all occurrences of $\tau$ is zero. Collapsed triangles are allowed in the map and contribute zero.
  - Explanation: Folding preserves each target-cell signed count and the Euler contradiction forces all such counts to vanish.
- **C17** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-controlled-diagrams)): Suppose that $T$ acts geometrically on $Y$ and fix $y\in Y$. There is a constant $C_1$, depending only on this action, $y$, and the fixed presentation, with the following property. Let $\Sigma$ be a triangulated parameter two-sphere, let $B$ be a subcomplex, and let $f:\Sigma\to Y$ be continuous. There is a refinement keeping $B$ a subcomplex and a combinatorial map from the refined sphere to $\mathcal K$ such that, for every final triangle $Q$, every one of its group-vertex labels $s$, and every $p\in Q$, $$\label{eq:all-points-control} d_Y(sy,f(p))\le C_1.$$ In particular $C_1$ is independent of $f$, its modulus of continuity, and the number of triangles required.
  - Explanation: A finite set of bounded boundary types yields diagram approximations with an all-points distance bound independent of the map.
- **C18** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-projected-approximation)): On $B$, the maps $q_n$ converge uniformly to $\kappa$.
  - Explanation: The all-points diagram bound yields uniform convergence of projected affine coordinates on the chosen patch.
- **C19** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-localized-degree)): For all sufficiently large $n$, every occurrence in the sphere diagram of a target triangle contributing to the winding count on $B$ lies entirely in $\operatorname{int}B$.
  - Explanation: Metric separation localizes every occurrence of a contributing labelled target triangle to the patch interior.
- **C20** (construction, [source](#stmt-def-matrix)): Let $\mathcal E=\{(a,b),(b,c),(c,a)\}$, as before, and set $$S=\{a,b,c,d,e\}\ \sqcup \{s_j^{uv}:(u,v)\in\mathcal E,\ 3\le j\le39\}.$$ All symbols in the second set are distinct. For each $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$, also write $s_1^{uv}=u$ and $s_2^{uv}=v$; these are aliases for existing elements of $S$. Define a symmetric matrix $M=(m_{st})_{s,t\in S}$ by the following prescriptions: 1. $m_{ss}=1$ for every $s\in S$, and $m_{ab}=m_{bc}=m_{ca}=3$. 2. In each block $\{s_1^{uv},\ldots,s_{39}^{uv}\}$, set $$m_{s_i^{uv},s_j^{uv}}= \begin{cases} 3,&\|i-j\|=1,\\ 2,&\|i-j\|\ge2. \end{cases}$$ 3. For $q\in\{d,e\}$ and $u\in\{a,b,c\}$, set $m_{qu}=2$. 4. Every remaining off-diagonal entry is $\infty$. Let $G=A_M$ be the resulting Artin group, with the generators denoted by the elements of $S$ themselves. Let $G_0$ be the group presented by the generators $S\setminus\{d,e\}$ and all their prescribed relations.
  - Explanation: The symmetric Artin matrix attaches braid blocks and adds generators that each commute with the triangle subgroup.
- **C21** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-pair-embeddings)): For every $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$, the homomorphism $$B_3=\langle t_1,t_2\mid t_1t_2t_1=t_2t_1t_2\rangle \longrightarrow T, \qquad t_1\longmapsto u,\quad t_2\longmapsto v,$$ is injective. For every $l\ge3$, the homomorphism $B_3\to B_l$ given by $t_1\mapsto s_1$ and $t_2\mapsto s_2$ is also injective.
  - Explanation: A retraction proves the pair embedding into the triangle group; a selected-token invariant proves the braid-group embedding.
- **C22** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-amalgam-normal-form)): Let $H_1,H_2$ be groups with a common embedded subgroup $E$, and let $H_1*_E H_2$ be the group obtained by identifying the two copies of $E$ in their free product. Each factor embeds in this group, and their intersection is $E$. A product $a_1\cdots a_n$, $n\ge1$, whose factors belong alternately to $H_1\setminus E$ and $H_2\setminus E$ is nontrivial. If such a product contains a factor from $H_i\setminus E$, it does not belong to the other factor.
  - Explanation: Alternating normal forms prove factor embeddings, the exact intersection and nontriviality of reduced products.
- **C23** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-common-centralizer)): For the elements $d,e$ of $G$, $$C_G(d)\cap C_G(e)=T.$$
  - Explanation: The simultaneous centralizer is the triangle subgroup; the added generators freely generate a free group.
- **C24** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-geometric-centralizer)): Suppose a group $H$ acts geometrically on a nonempty proper CAT(0) space $X$. For finitely many elements $q_1,\ldots,q_m\in H$, $m\ge1$, set $$C=\bigcap_{j=1}^m C_H(q_j).$$ If $A\ge0$ is finite and the set $$Y_A=\{x\in X:d_X(x,q_jx)\le A\text{ for }1\le j\le m\}$$ is nonempty, then $Y_A$, with its inherited metric, is a proper CAT(0) space, invariant under $C$, and the restricted $C$-action is geometric. Every element of $C$ has the same stable translation length in $Y_A$ as in $X$.
  - Explanation: A nonempty common displacement sublevel set supports a geometric centralizer action with inherited stable lengths.

```json
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-sector-data",
        "exact_quote": "Each of these six seminorms is positive definite. Each sector angle $\\theta$ satisfies $0<\\theta<\\pi/3$. It has a continuous injective map $v_*:P\\to E$ whose value in a sector for $U=u^2,H=z_{uv}$ is $$\\label{eq:separating-coordinates}\n v_*(\\lambda,\\mu)=\\lambda L_u+\\mu L_{uv},$$ and similarly with $v$ in place of $u$.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-sector-maps",
        "exact_quote": "There are continuous maps $f_n:P\\to Y$, with $f_n(0)=y$, and a constant $C_0$ independent of $n,p$, such that $$\\label{eq:grid-error}\n d_Y(f_n(p),t_n(p)y)\\le C_0.$$ For two points in any one sector, $$\\label{eq:sector-convergence}\n \\frac{d_Y(f_n(p),f_n(p'))}{n}\n \\longrightarrow\\|p-p'\\|_{U,H}$$ uniformly. In addition, uniformly on $P$, $$\\label{eq:radial-coordinates}\n \\frac{d_Y(y,f_n(p))}{n}\\longrightarrow r(p),\n \\qquad\n \\frac{V(t_n(p))}{n}\\longrightarrow v_*(p).$$ If $A_1,A_2\\subset P$ are disjoint nonempty closed sets, then for some $\\delta>0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, $$\\label{eq:region-separation}\n d_Y(f_n(A_1),f_n(A_2))\\ge\\delta n.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-254.2"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-controlled-diagrams",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose that $T$ acts geometrically on $Y$ and fix $y\\in Y$. There is a constant $C_1$, depending only on this action, $y$, and the fixed presentation, with the following property. Let $\\Sigma$ be a triangulated parameter two-sphere, let $B$ be a subcomplex, and let $f:\\Sigma\\to Y$ be continuous. There is a refinement keeping $B$ a subcomplex and a combinatorial map from the refined sphere to $\\mathcal K$ such that, for every final triangle $Q$, every one of its group-vertex labels $s$, and every $p\\in Q$, $$\\label{eq:all-points-control}\n  d_Y(sy,f(p))\\le C_1.$$ In particular $C_1$ is independent of $f$, its modulus of continuity, and the number of triangles required.",
        "source_id": "PM-254.2"
      },

        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal E=\\{(a,b),(b,c),(c,a)\\}$, as before, and set $$S=\\{a,b,c,d,e\\}\\ \\sqcup\n   \\{s_j^{uv}:(u,v)\\in\\mathcal E,\\ 3\\le j\\le39\\}.$$ All symbols in the second set are distinct. For each $(u,v)\\in\\mathcal E$, also write $s_1^{uv}=u$ and $s_2^{uv}=v$; these are aliases for existing elements of $S$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-amalgam-normal-form",
        "exact_quote": "Let $H_1,H_2$ be groups with a common embedded subgroup $E$, and let $H_1*_E H_2$ be the group obtained by identifying the two copies of $E$ in their free product. Each factor embeds in this group, and their intersection is $E$. A product $a_1\\cdots a_n$, $n\\ge1$, whose factors belong alternately to $H_1\\setminus E$ and $H_2\\setminus E$ is nontrivial. If such a product contains a factor from $H_i\\setminus E$, it does not belong to the other factor.",
        "source_id": "PM-254.2"
      },
      "evidence": [

        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-geometric-centralizer",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose a group $H$ acts geometrically on a nonempty proper CAT(0) space $X$. For finitely many elements $q_1,\\ldots,q_m\\in H$, $m\\ge1$, set $$C=\\bigcap_{j=1}^m C_H(q_j).$$ If $A\\ge0$ is finite and the set $$Y_A=\\{x\\in X:d_X(x,q_jx)\\le A\\text{ for }1\\le j\\le m\\}$$ is nonempty, then $Y_A$, with its inherited metric, is a proper CAT(0) space, invariant under $C$, and the restricted $C$-action is geometric. Every element of $C$ has the same stable translation length in $Y_A$ as in $X$.",
        "source_id": "PM-254.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-geometric-centralizer",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose a group $H$ acts geometrically on a nonempty proper CAT(0) space $X$. Every element of $C$ has the same stable translation length in $Y_A$ as in $X$.",
          "source_id": "PM-254.2"
        }
      ],

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-geometric-centralizer",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose a group $H$ acts geometrically on a nonempty proper CAT(0) space $X$. For finitely many elements $q_1,\\ldots,q_m\\in H$, $m\\ge1$, set $$C=\\bigcap_{j=1}^m C_H(q_j).$$ If $A\\ge0$ is finite and the set $$Y_A=\\{x\\in X:d_X(x,q_jx)\\le A\\text{ for }1\\le j\\le m\\}$$ is nonempty, then $Y_A$, with its inherited metric, is a proper CAT(0) space, invariant under $C$, and the restricted $C$-action is geometric. Every element of $C$ has the same stable translation length in $Y_A$ as in $X$.",
          "source_id": "PM-254.2"
        }
      ],
      "id": "C24",
      "kind": "conditional_result",
      "quantifiers": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-geometric-centralizer",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose a group $H$ acts geometrically on a nonempty proper CAT(0) space $X$. For finitely many elements $q_1,\\ldots,q_m\\in H$, $m\\ge1$, set $$C=\\bigcap_{j=1}^m C_H(q_j).$$ If $A\\ge0$ is finite and the set $$Y_A=\\{x\\in X:d_X(x,q_jx)\\le A\\text{ for }1\\le j\\le m\\}$$ is nonempty, then $Y_A$, with its inherited metric, is a proper CAT(0) space, invariant under $C$, and the restricted $C$-action is geometric. Every element of $C$ has the same stable translation length in $Y_A$ as in $X$.",
          "source_id": "PM-254.2"
        }
      ],
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Geometric action** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Throughout, an action is called *geometric* if it is an isometric action on a nonempty proper CAT(0) metric space $X$, is proper, and is cocompact.
- **Stable length** ([source](#eq:stable-length)): For an isometry $g$ and a base point $o$, define its *stable length* by $$\label{eq:stable-length} \ell(g)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{d(o,g^n o)}{n}.$$
- **Stable-length pairing** ([source](#stmt-lem-commuting-form)): For commuting $g,h$, put $$\label{eq:pairing-definition} \langle g,h\rangle =\frac{\ell(gh)^2-\ell(g)^2-\ell(h)^2}{2}.$$
- **Recursive braid centers** ([source](#sec:braids)): For a general $B_l$ with $l\ge3$, define $$\label{eq:braid-centers-definition} L_1=1,\qquad L_j=s_{j-1}L_{j-1}s_{j-1},\qquad Z_r=L_1\cdots L_r,\qquad N_r=\binom r2.$$
- **Triangle group and cyclic pairs** ([source](#sec:sectors)): Set $$T=\langle a,b,c\mid aba=bab,\ bcb=cbc,\ cac=aca\rangle,\qquad \mathcal E=\{(a,b),(b,c),(c,a)\},$$ and write $z_{uv}=(uv)^3$ for $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$.
- **Sector labels** ([source](#pm-h-10)): In each sector define the group label $$\label{eq:sector-label} t_n(p)=U^{\lfloor n\lambda\rfloor}H^{\lfloor n\mu\rfloor}.$$
- **Mirror coordinate projection** ([source](#eq:projection-identity)): For $\xi=\sum_D\xi_D e_D$, define the bounded linear map $$\label{eq:coordinate-projection} J\xi=\left(\frac{\xi_{D_u}-\xi_{D_{uv}}}{2}, \frac{\xi_{D_{uv}}}{2}\right).$$
- **Finite Artin matrix** ([source](#stmt-def-matrix)): Let $\mathcal E=\{(a,b),(b,c),(c,a)\}$, as before, and set $$S=\{a,b,c,d,e\}\ \sqcup \{s_j^{uv}:(u,v)\in\mathcal E,\ 3\le j\le39\}.$$

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| generator-count | Cardinality of the counterexample generator set | = | $116$ | All generators in the prescribed set. |
| private-block-count | Generators added per braid attachment | = | $37$ | Each attached block sharing its first two standard generators. |
| braid-ratio-square | Squared normalized central translation length | ≥ | $\frac{143}{513}$ | Elements satisfying the stated braid relations with positive squared-generator length. |
| braid-ratio-threshold | Normalized central translation length | > | $\frac12$ | The initial adjacent braid generators. |
| sector-angle-upper | Individual sector angle | < | $\pi/3$ | Each of the six sectors under the strict ratio hypotheses. |
| total-angle-upper | Total cyclic sector angle | < | $2\pi$ | The cyclic union of all six sectors. |
| half-strand-index | Half-strand parameter | = | $20$ | The final arithmetic specialization. |
| sphere-euler-characteristic | Euler characteristic of the dual sphere | = | $2$ | A component with nodes and no fold. |
| outer-cap-coefficient | Chosen positive outer-cap separation coefficient | = | $\tfrac12(1-\sin(\Omega/4))$ | Strict total-angle bound and sufficiently large dilation. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **stable-form** [claim](#stmt-lem-commuting-form) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-commuting-form): Quadrilateral comparison and polarization. -> A positive semidefinite quadratic form.
- **character** [claim](#stmt-lem-centralizer-character) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-centralizer-character): A limiting distance function defines a centralizer character. -> Additivity on the entire centralizer.
- **braid-bound** [claim](#stmt-prop-braid-angle) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-braid-centers-2): Center recursion and positivity for commuting subblocks. -> The strict normalized length bound.
- **sector-disk** [claim](#stmt-lem-sector-data) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-sector-data): Mirror-coordinate independence and cyclic sector gluing. -> A short-angle disk with injective separating coordinates.
- **sector-maps** [claim](#stmt-lem-sector-maps) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-sector-maps): Geodesic interpolation and finite rational nets. -> Uniform asymptotic maps and region separation.
- **outer-cap** [claim](#stmt-lem-outer-cap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-outer-cap): Boundary chord estimates and geodesic coning. -> A sphere map with a distant second disk.
- **signed-counts** [claim](#stmt-prop-sphere-counts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-sphere-counts): Delete opposite folds and apply the Euler contradiction. -> Zero signed count for every target triangle.
- **controlled-diagrams** [claim](#stmt-lem-controlled-diagrams) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-controlled-diagrams): Fix fillings of finitely many bounded boundary types. -> A refined sphere diagram with an all-points distance bound.
- **projection** [claim](#stmt-lem-projected-approximation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-projected-approximation): Project mirror coordinates and interpolate affinely. -> Uniform approximation on the coordinate patch.
- **localization** [claim](#stmt-lem-localized-degree) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-localized-degree): Use common target vertex labels and metric separation. -> All occurrences of contributing cells lie in the patch interior.
- **triangle-obstruction** [claim](#stmt-prop-triangle-obstruction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-localized-degree-2): Compare nonzero local winding with whole-sphere signed counts. -> The strict-ratio geometric triangle action is impossible.
- **embeddings** [claim](#stmt-lem-pair-embeddings) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pair-embeddings): Retraction for the triangle group and selected-token invariant for braid groups. -> Injective attaching subgroup maps.
- **common-centralizer** [claim](#stmt-lem-common-centralizer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-common-centralizer): Amalgam normal forms and free-word cancellation. -> The simultaneous centralizer equals the triangle subgroup.
- **centralizer-action** [claim](#stmt-lem-geometric-centralizer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-geometric-centralizer): Convexity and finite conjugacy tuples give cocompactness. -> A geometric centralizer action with unchanged stable lengths.
- **final-contradiction** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-geometric-centralizer-2): Restrict to the common sublevel set and transfer the braid inequalities. -> No geometric action of the constructed Artin group.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **matrix-prescriptions**: The symmetric matrix has diagonal labels $1$, the prescribed braid and commutation labels, and all remaining off-diagonal labels $\infty$.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **matrix-not-action**: Treat a correctly constructed matrix as a computational proof of the geometric obstruction. -> The finite label table does not establish the stable-length, sector, diagram, or centralizer arguments.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The explicit finite Artin matrix is constructive. This checks its prescriptions and does not certify the nonexistence of a geometric action.

```python
# INPUTS: integer n giving a leading principal submatrix size.
# PRECONDITIONS: 1 <= n <= 116; fixed ordering a,b,c,d,e, then ab,bc,ca private generators in increasing j.
# OUTPUTS: JSON n by n matrix; null denotes infinity.
# TIME: O(116**2) exact operations.
# SPACE: O(116**2) entries.
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers and FLINT fmpz.
# FAILURE: invalid input raises ValueError; no claim about CAT(0) actions is decided.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpz
n = int(sys.stdin.read())
if not 1 <= n <= 116:
    raise ValueError('size outside contract')
size = int(fmpz(5) + fmpz(3)*37)
m = [[None for _ in range(size)] for _ in range(size)]
for i in range(size):
    m[i][i] = 1
for b, (u,v) in enumerate(((0,1),(1,2),(2,0))):
    path = [u,v] + list(range(5+37*b,5+37*(b+1)))
    for i in range(39):
        for j in range(i+1,39):
            label = 3 if j-i == 1 else 2
            m[path[i]][path[j]] = m[path[j]][path[i]] = label
for q in (3,4):
    for u in (0,1,2):
        m[q][u] = m[u][q] = 2
print(json.dumps([row[:n] for row in m[:n]]))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **geometric-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The obstruction concerns proper, cocompact isometric actions on nonempty proper CAT(0) spaces. Blocked use: ['Do not extend the conclusion to arbitrary isometric actions or to actions lacking properness or cocompactness.']
- **existential-matrix** (counterexample_scope): The counterexample uses the explicit matrix in the construction; its rank and allowed labels alone do not identify that matrix. Blocked use: ['Do not conclude that every Artin group with these labels or this generator count has the same obstruction.']
- **braid-length-preconditions** (hypothesis): The quantitative braid bound requires a geometric ambient action, the specified braid relations, and positive stable length of the squared generator. Blocked use: ['Do not apply the ratio bound when the denominator or the braid relations are unproved.', 'Do not impose an additional requirement that the braid subgroup itself act cocompactly.']
- **strict-ratios** (edge_case): The triangle obstruction assumes the strict ratio inequality for every cyclic braid pair. Blocked use: ['Do not replace the strict inequality by equality or apply the contradiction from a single pair.']
- **centralizer-additivity** (hypothesis): Additivity with a fixed element holds on its centralizer under a geometric action; mutual commutation of the other factors is unnecessary. Blocked use: ['Do not expand the character on factors that do not centralize the fixed element.']
- **parameter-disk** (formal_scope): Euclidean sector metrics belong to the parameter construction; the cone disk is not asserted to be CAT(0). Blocked use: ['Do not identify the parameter disk with the unknown CAT(0) space.']
- **asymptotic-scale** (approximation): The sector convergence and localization arguments use sufficiently large dilation parameters with constants depending on the hypothetical action. Blocked use: ['Do not treat the limiting metric statements as exact identities at a finite dilation.', 'Do not infer a numerical termination threshold from existence of the constants.']
- **local-projection** (formal_scope): The planar projection recovers coordinates on the chosen sector, without a global injectivity claim. Blocked use: ['Do not use the planar projection to distinguish arbitrary points across the entire disk or sphere.']
- **target-cell-identities** (hypothesis): The winding contradiction counts individual labelled target cells with their translate information. Blocked use: ['Do not merge different target cells merely because projected vertex data coincide.']
- **all-points-control** (hypothesis): Localization requires the distance estimate at every point of every final parameter triangle. Blocked use: ['A vertex-only approximation does not supply the stated localization argument.']
- **free-centralizing-generators** (edge_case): The added generators centralize the triangle subgroup but do not commute with each other. Blocked use: ['Do not describe the added generators as commuting with each other.']
- **sublevel-nonempty** (hypothesis): The common-centralizer action is geometric on a nonempty simultaneous displacement sublevel set with a finite bound. Blocked use: ['Do not apply the restricted-action conclusion to an empty sublevel set.', 'Do not assume that the centralized elements must commute.']
- **finite-construction-scope** (formal_scope): The matrix prescriptions give finite constructive data; producing those data does not alone verify the geometric nonexistence argument. Blocked use: ['Do not present a finite matrix check as a certificate of the full CAT(0) obstruction.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 254 (`lean/docs/254.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Harmonic heights and the Artin $K(\pi,1)$ conjecture](../../preprints/Harmonic-heights-and-the-Artin-K-pi-1-conjecture-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [An Artin group with no geometric $\mathrm{CAT}(0)$ action](../../preprints/An-Artin-group-with-no-geometric-CAT-0-action-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Parabolic Intersections in Artin Groups](../../preprints/Parabolic-intersections-in-Artin-groups-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The Artin $K(\pi,1)$ conjecture asserts that the standard Salvetti complex of every finite-rank Artin group is aspherical. The formalized result proves the equivalent statement that its universal cover is contractible.
>
> It applies to every Coxeter matrix on a finite generating set, including empty and disconnected diagrams and infinite edge labels. The associated Coxeter and Artin groups may be infinite. Later corollaries in the manuscript are not included.
>
> The geometric-action question asks whether every Artin group acts properly and cocompactly by isometries on a proper CAT(0) space. The formalized result gives a counterexample: an Artin group defined by a symmetric matrix on $116$ generators, with diagonal entries $1$ and off-diagonal entries in $\{2,3,\infty\}$.
>
> It admits no such action on any nonempty proper CAT(0) space, in any dimension.
>
> The formalization proves that the intersection of any family of parabolic subgroups of a finite-rank Artin group is parabolic. Every such intersection is already the intersection of at most the rank many members, and every subset has a unique parabolic closure. Both finite and infinite Coxeter labels are allowed.
>
> For irreducible Artin groups with an infinite Coxeter label, it also proves acylindrical hyperbolicity and weak malnormality of every proper parabolic subgroup. These are the intersection and structural consequences selected from the paper.

Comparator source `ArtinCAT0.lean` (2840 bytes, sha256 `81e60d5355278668a02d5a5b606867229e4ddf9b0abf2d060adf08b1f2af944e`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace ArtinCAT0

universe uS uG uX

abbrev Letters := Fin 116
abbrev Label := WithTop ℕ

def blockLetter (b : Fin 3) (i : Fin 39) : Letters :=
  if h₀ : i.val = 0 then ⟨b.val, by omega⟩
  else if h₁ : i.val = 1 then ⟨(b.val + 1) % 3, by omega⟩
  else ⟨5 + 37 * b.val + (i.val - 2), by omega⟩

def Adjacent (s t : Letters) : Prop :=
  ∃ (b : Fin 3) (i j : Fin 39), s = blockLetter b i ∧ t = blockLetter b j ∧
    (i.val + 1 = j.val ∨ j.val + 1 = i.val)

def Distant (s t : Letters) : Prop :=
  ∃ (b : Fin 3) (i j : Fin 39), s = blockLetter b i ∧ t = blockLetter b j ∧
    (i.val + 2 ≤ j.val ∨ j.val + 2 ≤ i.val)

def Sentinel (s t : Letters) : Prop :=
  ((s.val = 3 ∨ s.val = 4) ∧ t.val < 3) ∨
  ((t.val = 3 ∨ t.val = 4) ∧ s.val < 3)

noncomputable def explicitMatrix (s t : Letters) : Label := by
  classical
  exact if s = t then 1 else if Adjacent s t then 3
    else if Distant s t ∨ Sentinel s t then 2 else ⊤

def alternating {S : Type uS} (s t : S) : ℕ → FreeGroup S
  | 0 => 1
  | n + 1 => FreeGroup.of s * alternating t s n

def artinRelators {S : Type uS} (M : S → S → Label) : Set (FreeGroup S) :=
  {w | ∃ (s t : S) (n : ℕ), M s t = (n : Label) ∧
    w = alternating s t n * (alternating t s n)⁻¹}

abbrev ArtinGroup {S : Type uS} (M : S → S → Label) := PresentedGroup (artinRelators M)

def Segment {X : Type uX} [MetricSpace X] (x y : X) (γ : ℝ → X) : Prop :=
  γ 0 = x ∧ γ 1 = y ∧
    ∀ s ∈ Set.Icc (0 : ℝ) 1, ∀ t ∈ Set.Icc (0 : ℝ) 1,
      dist (γ s) (γ t) = |s - t| * dist x y

def CAT0 (X : Type uX) [MetricSpace X] : Prop :=
  (∀ x y : X, ∃ γ : ℝ → X, Segment x y γ) ∧
  ∀ (x y z : X) (γ η : ℝ → X), Segment x y γ → Segment x z η →
    ∀ t ∈ Set.Icc (0 : ℝ) 1, ∀ u ∈ Set.Icc (0 : ℝ) 1,
      dist (γ t) (η u) ^ 2 ≤
        (t ^ 2 - t * u) * dist x y ^ 2 +
        (u ^ 2 - t * u) * dist x z ^ 2 + t * u * dist y z ^ 2

def GeometricAction (G : Type uG) (X : Type uX) [Group G] [MetricSpace X] [MulAction G X] : Prop :=
  CAT0 X ∧
  (∀ g : G, Isometry (fun x : X => g • x)) ∧
  (∀ K : Set X, IsCompact K → {g : G | ((fun x : X => g • x) '' K ∩ K).Nonempty}.Finite) ∧
  ∃ K : Set X, IsCompact K ∧ ∀ x : X, ∃ g : G, ∃ k ∈ K, g • k = x

universe u

theorem main :
    (∀ s t : Letters, explicitMatrix s t = explicitMatrix t s) ∧
    (∀ s : Letters, explicitMatrix s s = 1) ∧
    (∀ s t : Letters, s ≠ t →
      explicitMatrix s t = 2 ∨ explicitMatrix s t = 3 ∨ explicitMatrix s t = ⊤) ∧
    (∀ (X : Type u) [MetricSpace X] [ProperSpace X] [Nonempty X]
      [MulAction (ArtinGroup explicitMatrix) X],
      ¬ GeometricAction (ArtinGroup explicitMatrix) X) := by
  sorry

end ArtinCAT0

end OAI
```

The formalized result proves the equivalent statement that its universal cover is contractible.\n\nIt applies to every Coxeter matrix on a finite generating set, including empty and disconnected diagrams and infinite edge labels. <a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct an Artin group on 116 generators that admits no proper, cocompact isometric action on a nonempty proper CAT(0) space. This refutes the CAT(0) conjecture for Artin groups.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $S$ be finite and let $M=(m_{st})_{s,t\in S}$ be symmetric, with $m_{ss}=1$ and $$m_{st}\in\{2,3,\ldots\}\cup\{\infty\}\qquad(s\ne t).$$ The Artin group $A_M$ has generators $\sigma_s$, $s\in S$. For each finite $m_{st}$, its defining relation equates the two alternating words of length $m_{st}$ starting with $\sigma_s$ and $\sigma_t$. A label $\infty$ imposes no relation.

Throughout, an action is called *geometric* if it is an isometric action on a nonempty proper CAT(0) metric space $X$, is proper, and is cocompact. Here a metric space is proper when its closed bounded subsets are compact; action properness means that $$\{g:gK\cap K\ne\varnothing\}$$ is finite for every compact $K\subset X$; and cocompactness means that $GK=X$ for some compact $K$. Our CAT(0) convention is the geodesic triangle-comparison inequality with Euclidean comparison triangles. The explicit properness assumption on $X$ also follows from the other action hypotheses for a length space <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999), Exercise I.8.4(1)].

Charney asked which Artin groups are CAT(0) . The affirmative expectation that every finite-rank Artin group admits a geometric action is recorded by Haettel as Conjecture 1.1 of <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Haettel2022](#ref-Haettel2022)]. A geometric action would make the group’s large-scale geometry accessible through geodesic convexity and Euclidean comparison. The challenge is compatibility: constructing actions for the rank-two braid subgroups does not by itself supply an action of the whole group.

There are substantial positive results. Right-angled Artin groups, whose finite off-diagonal labels are all two, have finite nonpositively curved cubical classifying spaces and hence geometric CAT(0) actions <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[CharneyDavis1995](#ref-CharneyDavis1995), preprint Theorem 3.1.1]. Brady and McCammond constructed nonpositively curved two-dimensional complexes for the three-generator Artin groups with all three finite labels at least three <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[BradyMcCammond2000](#ref-BradyMcCammond2000), preprint Theorem 4]. Haettel proved the conjecture for XXL type, where every finite off-diagonal label is at least five <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Haettel2022](#ref-Haettel2022), Theorem 1.4]. For classical braid groups, Brady and McCammond established the five-strand case <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BradyMcCammond2010](#ref-BradyMcCammond2010), Theorem B], Haettel, Kielak, and Schwer the six-strand case <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[HaettelKielakSchwer2016](#ref-HaettelKielakSchwer2016), preprint Corollary 4.18], and Jeong the seven-strand case <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Jeong2023](#ref-Jeong2023), preprint Corollary 14].

In the opposite direction, Brady and Crisp found Artin groups with two-dimensional classifying spaces for which the least dimension of a geometric CAT(0) action is three <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BradyCrisp2002](#ref-BradyCrisp2002), preprint Theorems B and 3.1]. Their examples show that an obstruction in one dimension can disappear in a higher-dimensional CAT(0) space. To refute the conjecture, an obstruction must instead survive every dimension.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is an Artin matrix $M$ on 116 generators, with every off-diagonal label in $\{2,3,\infty\}$, such that $A_M$ admits no geometric action. One explicit matrix is given in Definition <a href="#def:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:matrix">20</a>.*

</div>

Thus Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives a negative resolution of the CAT(0) conjecture for Artin groups. In particular, the conclusion excludes every nonempty proper CAT(0) space, regardless of dimension or the choice of a complex or metric.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The construction and the obstruction

The small group that detects the obstruction is $$T=\langle a,b,c\mid aba=bab,\ bcb=cbc,\ cac=aca\rangle.$$ To each of its three cyclic braid pairs we attach a 40-strand braid group, sharing the first two standard generators of that block with the pair. Each attachment adds 37 generators. Two further generators $d,e$, each commuting with $a,b,c$ and having no other finite incident labels, make $T$ their simultaneous centralizer; Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">7</a> proves this equality. This gives $3+3\cdot37+2=116$ generators.

For an isometry coming from a geometric action, write $\ell(g)$ for its stable translation length, the limit of $d(x,g^n x)/n$. Section <a href="#sec:lengths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lengths">2</a> turns these lengths on commuting elements into Euclidean pairings and proves that a pairing with a fixed element is additive on its whole centralizer. The calculation in Section <a href="#sec:braids" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:braids">3</a> uses these standard geometric properties to constrain the braid relations, without requiring the braid blocks themselves to act cocompactly. A long block forces $$\frac{\ell((uv)^3)}{3\ell(u^2)}
 =\frac{\ell((uv)^3)}{3\ell(v^2)}>\frac12.$$ If these inequalities held on all three pairs in a geometric action of $T$, the commuting pairs $$(a^2,(ab)^3),\ (b^2,(ab)^3),\
 (b^2,(bc)^3),\ (c^2,(bc)^3),\
 (c^2,(ca)^3),\ (a^2,(ca)^3)$$ would give six asymptotically Euclidean sectors, each of angle less than $\pi/3$. Their cyclic union would have total angle less than $2\pi$.

A related angle-sum obstruction appears in Crisp’s study of two-dimensional Bestvina–Brady groups <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Crisp2002](#ref-Crisp2002), Lemma 1.3 and the following remark]. His argument assumes covering dimension two and a proper action in which each isometry attains its minimum displacement.

The main geometric step is to turn this short angular circuit into a contradiction that remains valid in arbitrary dimension. Coordinates coming from an affine mirror action separate disjoint regions of the sector disk at large scale. This action is the equilateral-triangle specialization of Digne’s reflection-coordinate homomorphism <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Digne2016](#ref-Digne2016), Proposition 2.1]. CAT(0) geodesics provide a second disk whose image stays far from the cone point. We transfer the resulting sphere to a triangular presentation complex, using a bound valid at every point of every parameter triangle. A local winding count then detects a target triangle with nonzero signed multiplicity, whereas a direct folding argument makes every such multiplicity zero.

The triangular presentation and its girth-six link are the $m=n=p=3$ specialization of Brady and McCammond’s construction <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BradyMcCammond2000](#ref-BradyMcCammond2000), preprint Lemmas 2–3]. We prove the facts needed here, including the signed-count and controlled-approximation statements. The latter comparison between local degree and global signed counts is formulated entirely in terms of distances and finite diagrams. It does not require a triangulation or a dimension bound on the unknown CAT(0) space.

Finally, the simultaneous-centralizer construction makes the action on $T$ geometric: a suitable intersection of two displacement sublevel sets is closed and convex, and $T$ acts cocompactly on it. Its stable lengths are the ambient stable lengths. The braid inequalities and the six-sector obstruction therefore apply to the same action.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Organization and notation

Sections <a href="#sec:lengths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lengths">2</a> and <a href="#sec:braids" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:braids">3</a> establish the length calculation. Sections <a href="#sec:sectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sectors">4</a>–<a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">6</a> prove the obstruction for $T$. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">7</a> gives the matrix, the algebraic embeddings, and the centralizer argument completing Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The substantial geometric, algebraic, and diagrammatic ingredients are proved below. We use elementary finite planar topology for regular neighbourhoods of graphs and winding numbers; no Artin-group asphericity theorem is an input.

We use $B_l$ for the braid presentation on $s_1,\ldots,s_{l-1}$: adjacent generators satisfy the length-three braid relation, and generators at distance at least two commute. For commuting elements $g,h$, the notation $\langle g,h\rangle$ will denote the polarization of their squared stable lengths, defined in Lemma <a href="#lem:commuting-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:commuting-form">4</a>.

<a id="sec:lengths"></a>
### Stable lengths and centralizer characters

Our objective is a pairing of stable lengths that is additive on an entire centralizer, even when its elements do not commute with one another. The geometric facts used here are standard: attainment of minimum displacement under a geometric action and the structure of its minimizing set are treated in Bridson–Haefliger <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999), II.6.8(5) and II.6.10(2)]; the Euclidean structure on commuting translations is central to the Flat Torus Theorem <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999), II.7.1]. We derive precisely the length identities needed for the braid calculation directly from triangle comparison, without using a splitting theorem.

<a id="stmt-lem-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:distance-convexity"></a>
<a id="eq:midpoint-comparison"></a>
<a id="eq:quadrilateral"></a>
**Lemma 2**. *In a CAT(0) space, geodesic segments are unique and depend continuously on their endpoints. If $\alpha,\beta:[0,1]\to X$ are geodesics parametrized proportionally to length, then $$\label{eq:distance-convexity}
 d(\alpha(t),\beta(t))
 \le (1-t)d(\alpha(0),\beta(0))+t d(\alpha(1),\beta(1)).$$ If $m$ is the midpoint of $[v,w]$, then $$\label{eq:midpoint-comparison}
 d(z,m)^2\le \frac{d(z,v)^2+d(z,w)^2}{2}
                    -\frac{d(v,w)^2}{4}.$$ For any four points $p,q,r,s$, $$\label{eq:quadrilateral}
 d(p,r)^2+d(q,s)^2
 \le d(p,q)^2+d(q,r)^2+d(r,s)^2+d(s,p)^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* For uniqueness, split one of two segments with the same endpoints at an arbitrary point and use the other segment as the third side of the resulting triangle. Its comparison triangle is a straight segment, so comparison identifies the two points with the same proportional parameter. For two segments with a common initial point, comparison bounds their distance at parameter $t$ by $t$ times the distance of their terminal points. The analogous statement holds for a common terminal point. Inserting the segment from $\alpha(0)$ to $\beta(1)$ between $\alpha$ and $\beta$, and using the triangle inequality, proves <a href="#eq:distance-convexity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-convexity">[eq:distance-convexity]</a>. This bound also proves continuous dependence on the endpoints; dependence on the parameter follows from constant-speed parametrization.

The Euclidean identity for a median, together with comparison, gives <a href="#eq:midpoint-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-comparison">[eq:midpoint-comparison]</a>. Let $m$ now be the midpoint of $[p,r]$. The triangle inequality and $(A+B)^2\le 2A^2+2B^2$ give $$d(q,s)^2\le 2d(q,m)^2+2d(s,m)^2.$$ Apply <a href="#eq:midpoint-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-comparison">[eq:midpoint-comparison]</a> to each term on the right to obtain <a href="#eq:quadrilateral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadrilateral">[eq:quadrilateral]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:stable-length"></a>
<a id="eq:length-powers"></a>
For an isometry $g$ and a base point $o$, define its *stable length* by $$\label{eq:stable-length}
 \ell(g)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{d(o,g^n o)}{n}.$$ Here the limit exists: the sequence $a_n=d(o,g^n o)$ is subadditive, and, for fixed $k$ and $n=qk+r$ with $0\le r<k$, $a_n\le q a_k+a_r$. Consequently $\limsup a_n/n\le a_k/k$ for every $k$, and the limit equals $\inf_{k\ge1}a_k/k$. Changing $o$ changes $a_n$ by at most twice the distance between the two base points. Thus the length is independent of $o$, is invariant under conjugation, and satisfies $$\label{eq:length-powers}
 \ell(g^m)=|m|\ell(g)\qquad(m\in\mathbb Z).$$ For negative $m$, use $d(o,g^{-n}o)=d(o,g^n o)$; for positive $m$, use the subsequence indexed by multiples of $m$ in <a href="#eq:stable-length" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stable-length">[eq:stable-length]</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-semisimple"></a>
<div id="lem:semisimple" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *Suppose $G$ acts geometrically on $X$. Every $g\in G$ attains its minimum displacement, and $$\ell(g)=\min_{x\in X}d(x,gx).$$ If this number is positive, $g$ preserves a geodesic line and translates along it by $\ell(g)$. Moreover, $\ell(g)=0$ if and only if $g$ has finite order.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-semisimple"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-semisimple</summary>

*Proof.* Set $D=\inf_xd(x,gx)$, and choose $x_n$ with $d(x_n,gx_n)\to D$. Write $x_n=h_n p_n$ with $p_n$ in a compact set $K$ whose $G$-translates cover $X$. After discarding finitely many terms, $q_n=h_n^{-1}gh_n$ moves $p_n$ by at most $D+1$. The closed $(D+1)$-neighborhood $K'$ of $K$ is closed and bounded, hence compact. Both $p_n$ and $q_np_n$ belong to $K'$, so $q_nK'\cap K'\ne\varnothing$. Action properness gives only finitely many possibilities for $q_n$. Pass to a subsequence on which $q_n=q$ and $p_n\to p\in K$. Then $d(p,qp)=D$. A fixed one of the conjugators $h_n$ transfers $p$ to a minimizer for $g$.

Let $x$ be such a minimizer. If $D>0$, let $m$ be the midpoint of $[x,gx]$; then $gm$ is the midpoint of $[gx,g^2x]$. Comparison in the triangle with vertices $x,gx,g^2x$ gives $$D\le d(m,gm)\le \tfrac12 d(x,g^2x)\le D.$$ Thus $d(x,g^2x)=2D$. We verify that all consecutive orbit segments form a line. Suppose the concatenation from $x$ to $g^i x$ is geodesic. In the triangle with vertices $x,g^i x,g^{i+1}x$, the points at distance $D$ from $g^i x$ along the two incident sides are $g^{i-1}x$ and $g^{i+1}x$. Their distance is $2D$, by the preceding equality and translation by $g^{i-1}$. Their Euclidean comparison points can be at distance $2D$ only if the comparison angle at $g^i x$ is $\pi$. Hence $d(x,g^{i+1}x)=(i+1)D$. Induction, followed by translation by all powers of $g$, proves that the segments $[g^i x,g^{i+1}x]$, $i\in\mathbb Z$, form an isometrically parametrized line. In particular $\ell(g)=D$.

If $D=0$, the minimizer is fixed, so $\ell(g)=0$. Its stabilizer is finite by action properness applied to the compact singleton $\{x\}$; thus $g$ has finite order. Conversely, finite order immediately gives zero stable length. ◻

</details>

For commuting isometries of a complete CAT(0) space, Rodenhausen <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Rodenhausen2013](#ref-Rodenhausen2013), Theorem 14.6] proves a parallelogram identity for infimal displacements. The next proof works directly with stable lengths.

<a id="stmt-lem-commuting-form"></a>
<div id="lem:commuting-form" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:pairing-definition"></a>
**Lemma 4**. *For pairwise commuting isometries $g_1,\ldots,g_k$ of a CAT(0) space, the function $$v\longmapsto \ell(g^v),\qquad
 g^v=g_1^{v_1}\cdots g_k^{v_k},\qquad v\in\mathbb Z^k,$$ extends uniquely to a continuous seminorm on $\mathbb R^k$ whose square is a positive semidefinite quadratic form. For commuting $g,h$, put $$\label{eq:pairing-definition}
 \langle g,h\rangle
   =\frac{\ell(gh)^2-\ell(g)^2-\ell(h)^2}{2}.$$ These pairings are the bilinear form on every commuting family; in particular, $\langle g^i,h^j\rangle=ij\langle g,h\rangle$. Lengths and pairings are invariant under simultaneous conjugation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-commuting-form"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-commuting-form</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\nu(v)=\ell(g^v)$ for integer $v$. Commutation, the triangle inequality, and <a href="#eq:length-powers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:length-powers">[eq:length-powers]</a> give $$\begin{gathered}
 \nu(u+v)\le\nu(u)+\nu(v),\qquad
 \nu(mv)=|m|\nu(v),\\
 |\nu(u)-\nu(v)|\le\sum_{i=1}^k|u_i-v_i|\ell(g_i).
\end{gathered}$$ Define $\nu(v/q)=\nu(v)/q$ for integers $q>0$. Homogeneity makes this well-defined on $\mathbb Q^k$, and the displayed Lipschitz bound gives a unique continuous extension to $\mathbb R^k$. It is a seminorm.

<a id="eq:parallelogram"></a>
Apply <a href="#eq:quadrilateral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadrilateral">[eq:quadrilateral]</a> to the four points $$o,\quad g^{nu}o,\quad g^{n(u+v)}o,\quad g^{nv}o$$ in that cyclic order. Divide by $n^2$ and pass to the limit to obtain, with $Q=\nu^2$, $$Q(u+v)+Q(u-v)\le 2Q(u)+2Q(v).$$ This initially holds for integer vectors and extends by scaling and continuity to real vectors. Apply it to $u+v,u-v$ and use $Q(2u)=4Q(u)$ to obtain the opposite inequality. Hence $$\label{eq:parallelogram}
 Q(u+v)+Q(u-v)=2Q(u)+2Q(v).$$

For completeness, polarization here requires only elementary algebra. Set $B(u,v)=(Q(u+v)-Q(u-v))/4$. It is symmetric and continuous, and <a href="#eq:parallelogram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parallelogram">[eq:parallelogram]</a> gives $$B(u+w,v)+B(u-w,v)=2B(u,v).$$ Taking $w=u$ gives $B(2u,v)=2B(u,v)$, since $B(0,v)=0$. Next take $u=(a+b)/2$, $w=(a-b)/2$ to obtain additivity in the first variable. Integer and rational homogeneity follow, and continuity gives real homogeneity. Symmetry gives the same properties in the second variable. Finally $B(u,u)=Q(u)\ge0$, so $B$ is positive semidefinite; expanding $Q(u+v)$ recovers <a href="#eq:pairing-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pairing-definition">[eq:pairing-definition]</a>. Conjugation invariance follows from that of stable length. ◻

</details>

The following strengthening of bilinearity is crucial. The elements $h_1,h_2$ in its statement need not commute with one another. Real translation characters also underlie Bridson’s obstruction to proper semisimple actions on two-dimensional CAT(0) spaces <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Bridson2001](#ref-Bridson2001), Proposition 3.1].

<a id="stmt-lem-centralizer-character"></a>
<div id="lem:centralizer-character" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5**. *For a geometric action and a fixed $g\in G$, the function $$C_G(g)\longrightarrow\mathbb R,\qquad h\longmapsto\langle g,h\rangle$$ is a group homomorphism. Thus $\langle g,h_1h_2\rangle=\langle g,h_1\rangle+\langle g,h_2\rangle$ whenever both $h_1$ and $h_2$ commute with $g$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-centralizer-character"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-centralizer-character</summary>

*Proof.* If $\ell(g)=0$, positive semidefiniteness in Lemma <a href="#lem:commuting-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:commuting-form">4</a> implies $\langle g,h\rangle=0$ for every $h\in C_G(g)$: the nonnegative quadratic $\ell(g^t h)^2=2t\langle g,h\rangle+\ell(h)^2$, for integer $t$ of both signs, forces the coefficient to vanish.

Now let $D=\ell(g)>0$ and choose a minimizer $x$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:semisimple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:semisimple">3</a>, $d(x,g^n x)=nD$. Define $$b(z)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl(d(z,g^n x)-nD\bigr).$$ The terms decrease by the triangle inequality and are bounded below by $-d(z,x)$, so the limit exists. The function $b$ is $1$-Lipschitz.

<a id="eq:busemann-difference"></a>
Fix $h\in C_G(g)$ and $z,w\in X$. Put $p_n=g^n x$ and $q_n=h^{-1}g^n x$. Since $h$ commutes with $g$, $d(p_n,q_n)=d(x,h^{-1}x)$ is constant. Two orderings in <a href="#eq:quadrilateral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadrilateral">[eq:quadrilateral]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 \bigl|d(z,p_n)^2+d(w,q_n)^2-d(w,p_n)^2-d(z,q_n)^2\bigr|
 \le d(z,w)^2+d(p_n,q_n)^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Every long distance on the left is $nD+O(1)$. More precisely, $d(z,p_n)=nD+b(z)+o(1)$ and $d(z,q_n)=d(hz,p_n)=nD+b(hz)+o(1)$, and similarly for $w$. Dividing the inequality by $2nD$ and passing to the limit therefore gives $$\label{eq:busemann-difference}
 b(hz)-b(hw)=b(z)-b(w).$$ Consequently $\chi(h)=b(x)-b(hx)$ is a homomorphism on $C_G(g)$: apply <a href="#eq:busemann-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:busemann-difference">[eq:busemann-difference]</a> with $z=kx,w=x$ to compute $\chi(hk)=\chi(h)+\chi(k)$. Also $b(x)=0$, $b(gx)=-D$, and hence $\chi(g)=D$. Lipschitz continuity, applied to $h^n$, gives $$n|\chi(h)|=|b(x)-b(h^n x)|\le d(x,h^n x),
 \qquad\text{so } |\chi(h)|\le\ell(h).$$ Apply this last inequality to $g^t h$ for every integer $t$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:commuting-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:commuting-form">4</a>, $$(tD+\chi(h))^2
 \le t^2D^2+2t\langle g,h\rangle+\ell(h)^2.$$ After cancelling the quadratic terms, the inequality for arbitrarily large positive and negative $t$ forces $D\chi(h)=\langle g,h\rangle$. Thus the pairing is a multiple of the homomorphism $\chi$, as asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:braids"></a>
### A quantitative constraint from braid groups

Let $B_l$ denote the group on $s_1,\ldots,s_{l-1}$ with relations $$s_i s_{i+1}s_i=s_{i+1}s_i s_{i+1},\qquad
 s_i s_j=s_j s_i\quad\text{if }|i-j|\ge2.$$ Throughout this section the same notation may denote elements of another group satisfying these relations. No injectivity or cocompactness of the resulting braid subgroup is needed for the length estimates.

<a id="stmt-prop-braid-angle"></a>
<div id="prop:braid-angle" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6**. *Suppose a group acting geometrically on a CAT(0) space contains elements $s_1,\ldots,s_{39}$ satisfying the $B_{40}$ relations, with $\ell(s_1^2)>0$. Then <a id="eq:braid-angle-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:braid-angle-bound}
 \left(\frac{\ell((s_1s_2)^3)}{3\ell(s_1^2)}\right)^2
 \ge\frac{143}{513}>\frac14,$$ and in particular $$\frac{\ell((s_1s_2)^3)}{3\ell(s_1^2)}
 =\frac{\ell((s_1s_2)^3)}{3\ell(s_2^2)}>\frac12.$$*

</div>

For $B_4$, Crisp and Paoluzzi compute translation components along an axis of a central element under a minimal geometric action <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[CrispPaoluzzi2005](#ref-CrispPaoluzzi2005), Proposition 5]; minimality means that no proper nonempty closed convex subset is invariant.

To prove Proposition <a href="#prop:braid-angle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:braid-angle">6</a>, we compare central elements of the initial braid subblocks using the centralizer characters of Section <a href="#sec:lengths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lengths">2</a>. For a general $B_l$ with $l\ge3$, define <a id="eq:braid-centers-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:braid-centers-definition}
 L_1=1,\qquad L_j=s_{j-1}L_{j-1}s_{j-1},\qquad
 Z_r=L_1\cdots L_r,\qquad N_r=\binom r2.$$ Here $2\le j\le l$ and $1\le r\le l$.

<a id="stmt-lem-braid-centers"></a>
<div id="lem:braid-centers" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:braid-shift"></a>
<a id="eq:braid-central-pairing"></a>
**Lemma 7**. *The element $L_j$ commutes with $s_i$ for $i\le j-2$, and $Z_r$ commutes with every $s_i$ for $i<r$. In particular, $$Z_2=s_1^2,\qquad Z_3=(s_1s_2)^3.$$ The element $\delta=s_1\cdots s_{l-1}$ satisfies $$\label{eq:braid-shift}
 \delta s_i\delta^{-1}=s_{i+1}\qquad(1\le i\le l-2).$$ In any geometric action of the ambient group, $$\label{eq:braid-central-pairing}
 \langle s_i^2,Z_r\rangle=\frac{\ell(Z_r)^2}{N_r}
 \qquad(2\le r\le l,\ 1\le i<r).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-braid-centers"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-braid-centers</summary>

*Proof.* Induct on $j$ for the assertion about $L_j$. For $i\le j-3$, both $s_{j-1}$ and $L_{j-1}$ commute with $s_i$. For $i=j-2$, put $a=s_{j-2}$, $b=s_{j-1}$, and $C=L_{j-2}$. Then $bC=Cb$ and $L_j=baCab$, so the braid relation gives $$aL_j=abaCab=babCab=baCbab=baCaba=L_j a.$$ This includes the case $j=3$, where $C=L_1=1$; the case $j=2$ is empty. In particular, $L_j$ commutes with each earlier $L_i$, because the word for $L_i$ uses only $s_1,\ldots,s_{i-1}$.

Next induct on $r$ for $Z_r$. Both $Z_{r-1}$ and $L_r$ commute with $s_i$ for $i\le r-2$. The only remaining generator is $s=s_{r-1}$. It commutes with $Z_{r-2}$. With $L=L_{r-1}$ we have $L_r=sLs$, and the already proved commutation of $L$ with $L_r$ says $LsLs=sLsL$. Therefore $$s(LL_r)=sLsLs=LsLs^2=(LL_r)s,$$ which proves the assertion. The case $r=2$ is immediate. Finally $$Z_3=s_1^2s_2s_1^2s_2
     =s_1(s_1s_2s_1)s_1s_2
     =s_1(s_2s_1s_2)s_1s_2=(s_1s_2)^3.$$

To verify <a href="#eq:braid-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:braid-shift">[eq:braid-shift]</a>, in $\delta s_i$ commute the last $s_i$ left past $s_{i+2},\ldots,s_{l-1}$, replace $s_i s_{i+1}s_i$ by $s_{i+1}s_i s_{i+1}$, and commute the new $s_{i+1}$ left past $s_1,\ldots,s_{i-1}$. The result is $s_{i+1}\delta$.

For the length identity, apply the character $h\mapsto\langle Z_r,h\rangle$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:centralizer-character" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:centralizer-character">5</a>. All $s_i$, $i<r$, are in its domain. Applying it to each adjacent braid relation shows that their values are equal; call the common value $\alpha$. The positive word for $L_j$ has $2(j-1)$ letters, so that for $Z_r$ it has $2N_r$ letters. Thus $$\ell(Z_r)^2=\langle Z_r,Z_r\rangle=2N_r\alpha,
 \qquad \langle s_i^2,Z_r\rangle=2\alpha,$$ which proves <a href="#eq:braid-central-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:braid-central-pairing">[eq:braid-central-pairing]</a>, including when $\ell(Z_r)=0$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-braid-centers-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-braid-centers</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:braid-angle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:braid-angle">6</a>.* We retain $l=2k$ with $k\ge3$ until the final arithmetic, where $k=20$. Put $g=s_1^2$, $D=\ell(g)>0$, and $$R_r=\frac{\ell(Z_r)^2}{N_r^2D^2}\quad(2\le r\le 2k),\qquad
 c_0=\frac{\langle s_1^2,s_3^2\rangle}{D^2}.$$ We will express each normalized center length $R_r$ in terms of $R_3$ and $c_0$. Positivity for the commuting odd-indexed squares and for two disjoint block centers will then constrain these quantities and force $R_3>1/4$.

<a id="eq:nonadjacent-pairing"></a>
All squared generators have length $D$, by <a href="#eq:braid-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:braid-shift">[eq:braid-shift]</a>. We claim that every pair with $j-i\ge2$ has $$\label{eq:nonadjacent-pairing}
 \langle s_i^2,s_j^2\rangle=c_0D^2.$$ First fix $i=1$. All $s_j$ with $j\ge3$ commute with $g$; their adjacent braid relations, evaluated under the character of $g$, show that $\langle g,s_j\rangle$ is independent of $j\ge3$. This proves the claim for $i=1$. For general $i,j$, conjugate the pair $(s_1,s_{j-i+1})$ by $\delta^{i-1}$. Every successive use of <a href="#eq:braid-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:braid-shift">[eq:braid-shift]</a> has index at most $l-2$, so it sends the pair to $(s_i,s_j)$. Simultaneous conjugation invariance proves the claim.

<a id="eq:normalized-center-pairing"></a>
<a id="eq:braid-recursion"></a>
By <a href="#eq:braid-central-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:braid-central-pairing">[eq:braid-central-pairing]</a>, $$\label{eq:normalized-center-pairing}
 \langle g,Z_r\rangle=N_rR_rD^2.$$ We compute this character in a second way. Since $Z_3=gL_3$, $$\langle g,L_3\rangle=(3R_3-1)D^2.$$ For $j>3$ the recursive definition gives the word $$L_j=(s_{j-1}\cdots s_3)L_3(s_3\cdots s_{j-1}).$$ Every displayed factor belongs to $C_G(g)$, and $\langle g,s_i\rangle=c_0D^2/2$ for $i\ge3$. Keeping $L_3$ as one factor, the character therefore gives $$\langle g,L_j\rangle
   =\bigl(3R_3-1+(j-3)c_0\bigr)D^2\qquad(j\ge3).$$ In particular, this calculation does not expand the noncommuting $s_2$-letters inside $L_3$. Summing over the factors of $Z_r$, including $L_2=g$, and using <a href="#eq:normalized-center-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-center-pairing">[eq:normalized-center-pairing]</a>, yields $$\label{eq:braid-recursion}
 N_rR_r=1+(r-2)(3R_3-1)+\binom{r-2}{2}c_0
 \qquad(3\le r\le 2k).$$

<a id="eq:lower-cross-pairing"></a>
There are $k$ mutually commuting squares $s_1^2,s_3^2,\ldots,s_{2k-1}^2$. The squared length of their product, computed by Lemma <a href="#lem:commuting-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:commuting-form">4</a>, is $$kD^2+k(k-1)c_0D^2\ge0.$$ Consequently $$\label{eq:lower-cross-pairing}
 c_0\ge-\frac1{k-1}.$$

For the other constraint, compare $Z_k$ with $Z'_k=\delta^k Z_k\delta^{-k}$. Repeated application of <a href="#eq:braid-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:braid-shift">[eq:braid-shift]</a> sends its generators $s_1,\ldots,s_{k-1}$ to $s_{k+1},\ldots,s_{2k-1}$; the largest index to which the shift formula is applied is $2k-2$. Every generator in the first block commutes with every generator in the second, so $Z_k$ and $Z'_k$ commute, and they have equal length by conjugacy.

<a id="eq:disjoint-centers"></a>
Write their positive words as $Z_k=x_1\cdots x_{2N_k}$ and $Z'_k=y_1\cdots y_{2N_k}$. Use first the character of $Z'_k$, since each $x_p$ commutes with $Z'_k$, and then the character of each $x_p$, since each $y_q$ commutes with $x_p$. These are two separate valid character expansions, giving $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle Z_k,Z'_k\rangle
 &=\sum_{p=1}^{2N_k}\langle x_p,Z'_k\rangle
   =\sum_{p,q=1}^{2N_k}\langle x_p,y_q\rangle\\
 &= (2N_k)^2\frac{c_0D^2}{4}=N_k^2c_0D^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Here the factor $1/4$ follows by bilinearity from <a href="#eq:nonadjacent-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nonadjacent-pairing">[eq:nonadjacent-pairing]</a> applied to $x_p^2,y_q^2$. The nonnegative squared length of $Z_k(Z'_k)^{-1}$ is thus $2N_k^2D^2(R_k-c_0)$, proving $$\label{eq:disjoint-centers}
 R_k\ge c_0.$$

Put $r=k$ in <a href="#eq:braid-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:braid-recursion">[eq:braid-recursion]</a> and use <a href="#eq:disjoint-centers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:disjoint-centers">[eq:disjoint-centers]</a>. Since $N_k-\binom{k-2}{2}=2k-3$, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 3R_3
 &\ge 1-\frac1{k-2}+\frac{2k-3}{k-2}c_0\\
 &\ge 1-\frac{3k-4}{(k-1)(k-2)},
\end{aligned}$$ where the second inequality is <a href="#eq:lower-cross-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lower-cross-pairing">[eq:lower-cross-pairing]</a>. At $k=20$, this says $$3R_3\ge 1-\frac{56}{342}=\frac{143}{171},\qquad
 R_3\ge\frac{143}{513}>\frac14.$$ Finally $Z_3=(s_1s_2)^3$, $N_3=3$, and $\ell(s_2^2)=\ell(s_1^2)=D$. These identities give the claimed ratios. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:sectors"></a>
### Six asymptotic sectors

Set $$T=\langle a,b,c\mid aba=bab,\ bcb=cbc,\ cac=aca\rangle,\qquad
 \mathcal E=\{(a,b),(b,c),(c,a)\},$$ and write $z_{uv}=(uv)^3$ for $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$.

<a id="stmt-prop-triangle-obstruction"></a>
<div id="prop:triangle-obstruction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 8**. *There is no geometric action of $T$ such that, for every $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$, <a id="eq:strict-ratios"></a>$$\label{eq:strict-ratios}
 \frac{\ell(z_{uv})}{3\ell(u^2)}
 =\frac{\ell(z_{uv})}{3\ell(v^2)}>\frac12.$$*

</div>

The denominators are positive in every geometric action: sending each of $a,b,c$ to $1\in\mathbb Z$ shows that their squares have infinite order, so Lemma <a href="#lem:semisimple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:semisimple">3</a> applies. The equality of the two ratios also follows from conjugacy of adjacent braid generators. We prove the proposition in Section <a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">6</a>. In this section, assume for contradiction that such an action on $Y$ exists and fix $y\in Y$.

We will construct a parameter disk $P$ made of six Euclidean sectors and maps $f_n:P\to Y$, indexed by positive integers $n$. Within each sector, image distances divided by $n$ approach Euclidean distances. Mirror coordinates will ensure a second property: disjoint closed regions of $P$ have images separated by a positive multiple of $n$. The total sector angle below $2\pi$ then lets us fill the same boundary by a second disk whose image stays a positive multiple of $n$ from $y$. Sections <a href="#sec:diagrams" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:diagrams">5</a> and <a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">6</a> compare the resulting sphere with finite labelled diagrams to obtain the contradiction.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Orbit control and mirror coordinates

Let $d_T$ be the word metric for $a,b,c$ and their inverses. The following form of the Švarc–Milnor orbit comparison is standard; see <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999), I.8.18–19]. Its direct proof records the constants used to turn mirror-coordinate separation into metric separation.

<a id="stmt-lem-orbit-control"></a>
<div id="lem:orbit-control" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:orbit-control"></a>
**Lemma 9**. *For a geometric action of $T$ and any base point $y$, there are constants $R,C<\infty$ such that $Ty$ is $R$-dense and $$\label{eq:orbit-control}
 d_T(s,t)\le C\bigl(1+d_Y(sy,ty)\bigr)\qquad(s,t\in T).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-orbit-control"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-orbit-control</summary>

*Proof.* If $TK=Y$ with $K$ compact, take $R=\max_{p\in K}d(y,p)$. Every $tp$ is within $R$ of $ty$. For $L=d(sy,ty)$, divide the geodesic into $N=\max(1,\lceil L\rceil)\le L+1$ pieces, each of length at most one. Choose nearby orbit points at the division points, keeping the prescribed endpoint labels $s,t$. Each successive group increment $q$ satisfies $d(y,qy)\le2R+1$. There are finitely many such $q$: the compact ball $\overline B(y,2R+1)$ contains both $y$ and $qy$, so properness applies. The largest word length of these increments, enlarged to at least one, can be used for $C$. Summing their word lengths proves <a href="#eq:orbit-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orbit-control">[eq:orbit-control]</a>. ◻

</details>

Digne’s reflection-coordinate homomorphism sends an Artin generator associated to a reflection $r$ to $(e_r,r)$ in a semidirect product <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Digne2016](#ref-Digne2016), Proposition 2.1]. We use its concrete version for the side reflections of an equilateral triangle and verify its formulas. The resulting coordinates will distinguish all six sectors.

Take an equilateral triangle in the Euclidean plane, and denote its three side lines by $D_a,D_b,D_c$. Let $r_u$ be reflection in $D_u$, and let $\mathcal D$ be the set of all images of these three lines under the generated reflection group. Write $$E=\bigoplus_{D\in\mathcal D}\mathbb R e_D,\qquad
 \left\|\sum_D \xi_D e_D\right\|_1=\sum_D|\xi_D|.$$ All vectors here have finite support. Each $r_u$ preserves $\mathcal D$ and acts linearly on $E$ by permuting its basis vectors.

<a id="stmt-lem-mirror-action"></a>
<div id="lem:mirror-action" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:mirror-lipschitz"></a>
<a id="eq:mirror-translations"></a>
**Lemma 10**. *The formulas $$u\cdot\xi=e_{D_u}+r_u\xi\qquad(u=a,b,c)$$ define an affine isometric action of $T$ on $E$. If $V(t)=t\cdot0$, then $$\label{eq:mirror-lipschitz}
 \|V(t)-V(s)\|_1\le d_T(s,t).$$ For each $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$, the elements $u^2,z_{uv}$ act by pure translations with vectors $$\label{eq:mirror-translations}
 L_u=2e_{D_u},\qquad
 L_{uv}=2(e_{D_u}+e_{D_v}+e_{D_{uv}}),$$ where $D_{uv}$ is the third mirror at $D_u\cap D_v$. The three lines $D_{ab},D_{bc},D_{ca}$ are distinct from one another and from $D_a,D_b,D_c$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mirror-action"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mirror-action</summary>

*Proof.* Two incident side reflections generate the reflection group of three lines spaced at angles $\pi/3$ about their intersection. Their two length-three braid words have the same linear part. The translation part of $uvu$ is $$e_{D_u}+e_{r_uD_v}+e_{r_ur_vD_u}
 =e_{D_u}+e_{D_{uv}}+e_{D_v}.$$ For $vuv$, the three terms occur in the reverse order. Thus the translation parts also agree, proving the affine braid relations.

Each generator or inverse moves $0$ a vector of norm one, so a word of length $k$ moves $0$ a distance at most $k$. For the linear permutation part $r_s$ of $s$, the cocycle identity gives $$V(t)-V(s)=r_s V(s^{-1}t).$$ Taking norms proves <a href="#eq:mirror-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mirror-lipschitz">[eq:mirror-lipschitz]</a>.

Reflection in $D_u$ fixes its own basis vector, giving the translation vector $2e_{D_u}$ for $u^2$. Also $(uv)^3=(uvu)^2$. The affine map $uvu$ has translation vector equal to the sum of the three mirror vectors, and its linear part preserves that sum and has square one. Squaring proves the second formula in <a href="#eq:mirror-translations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mirror-translations">[eq:mirror-translations]</a>. The third mirror at a vertex of an equilateral triangle is parallel to the opposite side and distinct from it. The three opposite directions are different. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The parameter disk

For each pair $(u,v)\in\mathcal E$, use both commuting pairs $$(U,H)=(u^2,z_{uv}),\qquad (U,H)=(v^2,z_{uv}).$$ Commutation follows from Lemma <a href="#lem:braid-centers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:braid-centers">7</a>. On the exponent plane $\mathbb R^2$ of each pair, let $\|\cdot\|_{U,H}$ be the stable seminorm from Lemma <a href="#lem:commuting-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:commuting-form">4</a>. Give its nonnegative quadrant exponent coordinates $(\lambda,\mu)$, corresponding to $U,H$, respectively. The next lemma proves that this quadrant is a Euclidean sector.

<a id="stmt-lem-sector-data"></a>
<div id="lem:sector-data" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11**. *Each of these six seminorms is positive definite. Each sector angle $\theta$ satisfies $0<\theta<\pi/3$. The sectors glue isometrically along their shared rays, in the cyclic order <a id="eq:ray-order"></a>$$\label{eq:ray-order}
 a^2,\ z_{ab},\ b^2,\ z_{bc},\ c^2,\ z_{ca},\ a^2.$$ Their radius-one disk $P$ is a topological disk with total angle $0<\Omega<2\pi$. It has a continuous injective map $v_*:P\to E$ whose value in a sector for $U=u^2,H=z_{uv}$ is <a id="eq:separating-coordinates"></a>$$\label{eq:separating-coordinates}
 v_*(\lambda,\mu)=\lambda L_u+\mu L_{uv},$$ and similarly with $v$ in place of $u$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sector-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sector-data</summary>

*Proof.* For integral $(i,j)$, the pure translation formulas give $$V((U^iH^j)^m)=m(iL_u+jL_{uv}).$$ Use <a href="#eq:mirror-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mirror-lipschitz">[eq:mirror-lipschitz]</a> and <a href="#eq:orbit-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orbit-control">[eq:orbit-control]</a>, divide by $m$, and let $m\to\infty$. It follows that $$\|iL_u+jL_{uv}\|_1\le C\|(i,j)\|_{U,H}.$$ The same inequality holds on rational vectors by homogeneity and on real vectors by continuity. The two displayed translation vectors are linearly independent: the extra-mirror coordinate first determines $j$, and the $D_u$-coordinate then determines $i$. The stable form is therefore positive definite.

The central pairing formula <a href="#eq:braid-central-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:braid-central-pairing">[eq:braid-central-pairing]</a>, with $r=3$, gives $$\langle U,H\rangle=\frac{\ell(H)^2}{3}.$$ Hence <a id="eq:sector-angle"></a>$$\label{eq:sector-angle}
 \cos\theta=\frac{\langle U,H\rangle}{\ell(U)\ell(H)}
 =\frac{\ell(H)}{3\ell(U)}>\frac12.$$ Positive definiteness ensures $\cos\theta<1$. This proves the angle bounds.

Along a ray for an element $W$, exponent $t\ge0$ has distance $t\ell(W)$ from the origin in either adjacent sector. Thus the identifications preserve both exponents and metrics. The resulting cone is parametrized by a radius $r\ge0$ and a cyclic angle of period $\Omega$, with all angle values identified at $r=0$. Its part $0\le r\le1$ is the asserted disk.

The formulas for $v_*$ agree on every shared ray. If a vector in its image has a nonzero extra-mirror coordinate, that coordinate specifies $(u,v)$ and $\mu$. The excess over $2\mu$ on one of the original two side coordinates specifies the remaining exponent and which of the two sectors is used. If there is no excess, the point is on the shared $H$-ray. With no extra-mirror coefficient, the vector is on one squared-generator ray or is zero. These descriptions distinguish points precisely up to the stated ray identifications. They prove injectivity, including at the origin. ◻

</details>

We use only the Euclidean metric within each sector of $P$; we do not claim that this cone disk is CAT(0). Figure <a href="#fig:sectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:sectors">1</a> shows the cyclic gluing.

<figure id="fig:sectors">
<embed src="figures/sectors.pdf" />
<figcaption>The six sectors of the parameter disk $P$, cut open along the $a^{2}$-ray. The two boundary copies of that ray are identified. The angles and patch sizes are schematic; each angle is less than $π/3$, with total $Ω < 2π$. The chord is drawn after unfolding a shorter angular arc and illustrates Lemma <a href="#lem:outer-cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:outer-cap">13</a>; the nested patches $B′ ⊂ int B$ are chosen in Subsection <a href="#sec:local-patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local-patch">6.1</a> for the local winding count.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-sectors"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:sectors`)

The source figure is an external graphic; no image payload is embedded.

Visual witness description of PDF page 13 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A circular cone disk $P$, cut open into six triangular sectors radiating from the center origin labeled $0$. The six sector angles are labeled counterclockwise as $\theta_1, \theta_2, \theta_3, \theta_4, \theta_5, \theta_6$. The boundary rays of the sectors are labeled counterclockwise starting from the lower-right cut ray: $a^2$, $z_{ab}$, $b^2$, $z_{bc}$, $c^2$, $z_{ca}$, and the lower-left cut ray $a^2$. Below the cut boundary rays is an annotation with arrows: "identify the two $a^2$ rays". In sector $\theta_1$ between ray $a^2$ and ray $z_{ab}$, two nested quadrilateral patch regions are drawn: an outer patch labeled $B$ and an inner patch labeled $B'$. A red straight line segment labeled "chord" connects a point $p$ on the ray $z_{ab}$ at the outer perimeter to a point $p'$ on the ray $c^2$ at the outer perimeter.
```
